      2011 CONCEPT OF COMPUTER MCQ MARKING

   1. Who invented the Analytical Engine?
      úYaf,aIKd;aul tkaðu (Analytical Engine) fidhd .kq ,enqfõ ljqreka úiska o?

          1)    Blaise Pascal / íf,aia meial,a
          2)    Charles Babbage / pd¾,aia nefíÊ
          3)    John Von Neumann / fcdaka fjdka kshqudka
          4)    John V. Atanasoff / fcdaka ù' wgdkdfid*a
          5)    John Presper Eckert / fcdaka fm%iam¾ tl¾Ü

                                                                                                                                                         ( 2011 – 01 )
          Answer : 2 )

          Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
          Option/ úl,amh 1)
          Blaise Pascal was a French mathematician and physicist who invented the Pascaline, a mechanical
          calculator, in the 17th century. While his invention was important in the history of computing, it was not
          a programmable machine like the Analytical Engine.

          .Ks;{fhl= iy fN!;sl úoHd{fhls' mß.Kl b;sydifha Tyqf.a kj ksmehqu jeo.;a jqjo" th
          úYaf,aIK tkaðu fuka jevigyka.; l< yels hka;%hla fkdfõ'

          Option/ úl,amh 2)
          Charles Babbage is often referred to as the "Father of the Computer" because he conceptualized and
          designed the Analytical Engine in the 1830s. This was the first design of a general-purpose programmable
          computing machine. It included key features like the CPU, store (memory), input, and output, which
          inspired modern computer architecture.

          wdodkh iy m%;sodkh jeks m%Odk wx. we;=<;a úh'

          Option/ úl,amh 3)
          John Von Neumann contributed significantly to modern computer architecture, especially with the Von
          Neumann Architecture, which most computers still follow today. However, his work came more than 100
          years after Babbage and did not include inventing the Analytical Engine. He built on earlier ideas to
          develop practical and efficient computing systems.

          Option/ úl,amh 4)
          John V. Atanasoff, along with Clifford Berry, developed the Atanasoff–Berry Computer (ABC), one of
          the first electronic digital computers, in the 1930s. While this was a groundbreaking invention, it was
          different from the Analytical Engine, which was mechanical and conceptualized a century earlier.
          fcdaka ù' wegkdfid*a" la,s*¾â fnÍ iuÕ tlaj 1930 .Kkaj,§ m<uq bf,lafg%dksl äðg,a

    Option/ úl,amh 5)
    John Presper Eckert co-developed the ENIAC (Electronic Numerical Integrator and Computer) with John
    Mauchly in the 1940s. ENIAC was one of the first general-purpose electronic digital computers, marking
    a major advancement in computing.

 2. Which of the following technologies is used in the Second-Generation Computers?
    fojk mrïmrdfõ (Second generation) mß.Klj, Ndú; jkafka my; i|yka ljr ;dlaIKho?

    1)   Integrated Circuits / wkql,s; mßm: (ICs)
    2)   Large Scale Integration / uyd mßudK wkql, (LSI)
    3)   Microprocessors / laIqø ilik
    4)   Transistors / g%dkaisiagr
    5)   Vacuum Tubes / ßla;l k<

                                                                                                ( 2011 – 02 )
    Answer : 4 )
    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    Option/ úl,amh 1)
    Integrated Circuits (ICs) were introduced during the third generation of computers, not the second. ICs
    allowed multiple transistors to be placed on a single chip, which significantly improved computing speed
    and reduced size. However, this technology came after the second generation.
    wkql,s; mßm: ^IC& y÷kajd fokq ,enqfõ fojk mrïmrdfõ mß.Kl w;r;=r fkdj" ;=kajk
    mrïmrdfõ mß.Kl w;r;=r h' IC uÕska nyq g%dkaisiagr ;ks Ñmhla u; ;eîug bv ,nd ÿka w;r"
    tuÕska mß.Kl fõ.h ie,lsh hq;= f,i jeäÈhqKq jQ w;r m%udKh wvq úh' flfia fj;;a" fuu
    ;dlaIKh fojk mrïmrdfjka miqj meñKsfhah'

    Option/ úl,amh 2)
    Large Scale Integration (LSI) refers to a technology where thousands of transistors are integrated into a
    single chip. This advancement is part of the fourth generation of computers, which began in the 1970s.
    LSI made computers even smaller and more powerful, but it is not associated with second-generation
    computers.
    Large Scale Integration (LSI) hkq g%dkaisiagr oyia .Kkla ;ks Ñmhlg tAldnoaO lrk ,o ;dlaIKhls'
    fuu ÈhqKqj 1970 .Kkaj, wdrïN jQ isõjk mrïmrdfõ mß.Klj, fldgils' LSI mß.Kl Bg;a
    jvd l=vd yd n,j;a l< kuq;a th fojk mrïmrdfõ mß.Kl iuÕ iïnkaO fkdfõ'

    Option/ úl,amh 3)
    Microprocessors are the hallmark of the fourth generation of computers. Introduced in the 1970s, they
    combine the functions of a computer's central processing unit (CPU) on a single chip.
    laIqø ilikhka isõjk mrïmrdfõ mß.Klj, ,laIKhls' 1970 .Kkaj, y÷kajd ÿka tAjd
    mß.Klhl uOHu ieliqï tAllfha (CPU) ld¾hhka ;ks Ñmhla u; tAldnoaO lrhs'

    Option/ úl,amh 4)
    Transistors replaced vacuum tubes in the second generation of computers (roughly 1956–1963). They
    were much smaller, more reliable, used less power, and generated less heat than vacuum tubes. This
    technological shift marked a major improvement in computing efficiency and performance.
    g%dkaisiagr fojk mrïmrdfõ mß.Klj, ^wdikak jYfhka 1956-1963& ßla; k, m%;sia:dmkh lf<ah'
    tAjd fndfyda l=vd" jvd úYajdiodhl" wvq n,hla Ndú;d l< w;r ßla; k, j,g jvd wvq ;dmhla

    iksgqyka lf<ah'

    Option/ úl,amh 5)
    Vacuum tubes were used in first-generation computers (1940s–1950s). While they made early computing
    possible, they were large, unreliable, and consumed a lot of energy. The second generation moved away
    from vacuum tubes to transistors.
    m<uq mrïmrdfõ mß.Klj, ^1940-1950 .Kka& ßla; k, Ndú;d lrk ,§' tAjd uq,a mß.KllrKh
    l< yels jqjo" tAjd úYd," úYajdi l< fkdyels iy úYd, Yla;shla mßfNdackh lf<ah' fojk
    mrïmrdj ßla; k, j,ska g%dkaisiagr fj; udre úh'

    Therefore, option 4) is the correct answer.

 3. Consider the following properties:
    my; oelafjk ,laIK i,lkak'
    A. Density / >k;ajh
    B. Capacity / Odß;dj
    C. Security / wdrlaIdj
    D. Cost / msßjeh
    E. Access time / m%fõY ld,h
    Which of the above are being used as the main properties to classify different types of memory?
    by; ,laIK w;=frka úúO j¾.j, mß.Kl u;l j¾.SlrKh i|yd fhdod.efkk m%Odk ,laIK
    jkqfha

    1) B and D only. 2) A, B and C only. 3) B, D and E only. 4) A,B,C and D only.        5) A,B,D and E only.
       B yd D muKs.     A, B yd C muKs.     B, D yd E muKs.     A,B,C yd D muKs.            A,B,D yd E muKs.

                                                                                                 ( 2011 – 29 )
    Answer : 5 )
    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    Statement / m%ldYh A)
    Density refers to how much data can be stored in a given physical space on a memory chip. A higher
    density means more storage capacity in a smaller size, which is desirable in compact devices. This
    characteristic is often considered when evaluating or comparing different types of memory. It directly
    affects performance and design considerations. Therefore, density is a key classification factor.
    >k;ajh hkq u;l Ñmhla u; § we;s fN!;sl wjldYhl fldmuK o;a; .nvd l< yelso hkakhs'
    jeä >k;ajhla hkq l=vd m%udKhl jeä .nvd Odß;djla jk w;r th ixhqla; Wmdx.j, fhda.H fõ'
    úúO j¾.fha u;lhka we.hSfï§ fyda ixikaokh lsÍfï§ fuu ,laIKh fndfyda úg i,ld n,kq
    ,efí' th ld¾h idOkh iy ie,iqï i,ld ne,Sï j,g iDcqju n,mdhs' tneúka" >k;ajh m%Odk
    j¾.SlrK idOlhls'

    Statement / m%ldYh B)
    Capacity is one of the most important features of any memory type. It indicates how much data a memory
    unit can hold at a time. Different types of memory (RAM, ROM, cache, etc.) are classified partly based
    on their storage capacity. This helps in deciding the most suitable memory type for specific tasks or
    devices. So, capacity is a major classification property.
    ´kEu u;l j¾.hl Odß;djh jeo.;au ,laIKhls' u;l tAllhlg tljr fldmuK o;a; m%udKhla
    r|jd .; yelso hkak thska fmkakqï flf¾' úúO j¾.fha u;lhka ^RAM, ROM ksys; u;lh" wdÈh&
    tAjdfha .nvd Odß;dj u; mokïj w¾O jYfhka j¾.SlrKh lr we;' fuh ksYaÑ; ld¾hhka fyda
    Wmdx. i|yd jvd;a iqÿiq u;l j¾.h ;SrKh lsÍug WmldÍ fõ' tneúka" Odß;dj m%Odk j¾.SlrK
    .=Kdx.hls'

    Statement / m%ldYh C)
    Security deals with protection against unauthorized access or data loss. While important in overall
    computer systems, it is not a core factor used to classify memory types. Most memory classifications focus
    on physical and performance characteristics rather than security. Therefore, security is not considered a
    main classification property for memory.

    wdrlaIdj wkjir m%fõYh fyda o;a; ke;sùug tfrysj wdrlaIdj iuÕ lghq;= lrhs' iuia; mß.Kl
    moaO;sj, jeo.;a jqjo" th u;l j¾. j¾.SlrKh lsÍug Ndú;d lrk uQ,sl idOlhla fkdfõ'
    fndfyda u;l j¾.SlrKhka wdrlaIdjg jvd fN!;sl iy ld¾h idOk ,laIK flfrys wjOdkh fhduq
    lrhs' tneúka" wdrlaIdj u;lh i|yd m%Odk j¾.SlrK .=Kdx.hla f,i fkdie,fla'

    Statement / m%ldYh D)
    Cost is a major factor when selecting or comparing memory types. Faster and higher-capacity memory
    usually comes at a higher cost. Manufacturers and users often balance performance against cost when
    choosing between types like SSDs vs. HDDs, or DRAM vs. SRAM. Hence, cost is a widely used property
    in memory classification.
    u;l j¾. f;dard.ekSfï§ fyda ixikaokh lsÍfï§ msßjeh m%Odk idOlhls' fõ.j;a yd by<
    Odß;djhlska hq;a u;lh idudkHfhka by< msßjehlska hqla; fõ' ksIamdolhska iy mßYS,lhska fndfyda

    Statement / m%ldYh E)
    Access time defines how quickly data can be read from or written to memory. Faster access time leads to
    better system performance, especially in tasks requiring frequent memory operations. Memory types like
    cache, RAM, and secondary storage are often categorized based on their access time. Therefore, access
    time is a crucial classification metric.
    m%fõY ld,h hkq u;lhg o;a; lshúh yels fyda ,súh yels fõ.h ks¾jpkh lrhs' fõ.j;a m%fõY
    ld,h jvd fyd| moaO;s l%shdldß;ajhlg u. mdohs" úfYaIfhka ks;r u;l fufyhqï wjYH jk
    ld¾hhka j,§' ksys; u;lh" RAM iy oaú;shsl wdphkh jeks u;l j¾. fndfyda úg tAjdfha m%fõY
    ld,h u; mokïj j¾.SlrKh flf¾' tneúka" m%fõY ld,h ;SrKd;aul j¾.SlrK ñkquls'

    Therefore, the correct answer is 5) A, B, D and E only.
    tneúka" ksjerÈ ms<s;=r 5) A, B, D iy E muKs'

 4. Consider the following memory types:
    my; oelafjk u;l j¾. i,lkak'
    A. Read Only Memory / mGkud;% u;lh
    B. Secondary storage / oaú;shsl wdphkh
    C. Cache memory / ksys; u;lh
    D. Flash memory / iefk<s u;lh
    E. Random Access Memory / iiïNdù m%fõY u;lh
    Which of the above can be considered as Volatile Memories?
    kIH u;l (Volatile memories) jYfhka ie,lsh yelafla by; i|yka l=uk tajd o?

    1) A and B only.   2) A and C only.    3) C and D only. 4) C and E only.      5) D and E only.
       A yd B muKs.       A yd C muKs.        C yd D muKs.     C yd E muKs.          D yd E muKs.

                                                                                              ( 2011 – 30 )
    Answer : 4 )

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    Statement / m%ldYh A)
    ROM is a non-volatile memory, meaning it retains its data even when power is turned off. It is mainly
    used to store firmware or system-level instructions that don't change frequently. ROM is essential for
    booting up systems and does not lose its contents after shutdown. Hence, ROM is not considered volatile
    memory.
    ROM hkq kYH fkdjk u;lhls" tkï n,h wl%sh l< úg mjd tys o;a; r|jd .kS' th m%Odk
    jYfhka Ndú;d lrkqfha iaÓrdx. fyda ks;r fjkia fkdjk moaO;s uÜgfï Wmfoia .nvd lsÍug h'
    moaO;s wdrïN lsÍu i|yd ROM w;HjYH jk w;r jid oeófuka miq tys wka;¾.;h ke;s fkdfõ'
    tneúka" ROM kYH u;lh f,i fkdie,fla'

RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                                 -5-                                            #ictfromabc

    Statement / m%ldYh B)
    Secondary storage includes devices like hard drives, SSDs, CDs, and USB drives. These storage types are
    non-volatile, designed specifically to store data permanently until manually deleted. They are used for
    long-term data storage and are not wiped on power loss. Therefore, secondary storage is not volatile.
    oaú;shsl .nvdjg oDV ;eá" SSD, CD iy USB Odjl jeks Wmdx. we;=<;a fõ' fuu .nvd j¾.
    kYH fkdjk w;r" w;ska uld ouk f;la iaÓrju o;a; .nvd lsÍu i|yd úfYaIfhka ks¾udKh lr
    we;' tAjd È.= ld,Sk o;a; .nvd lsÍu i|yd Ndú;d lrk w;r n,h ke;sùfï§ uld fkdoukq ,efí'
    tneúka" oaú;shsl .nvdj kYH fkdfõ'

    Statement / m%ldYh C)
    Cache memory is a small, high-speed type of memory located close to the CPU. It temporarily stores
    frequently accessed data and instructions for quick access. However, cache is volatile, which means all
    data stored in it is lost when the system is powered off. So, cache memory is correctly identified as volatile.
    ksys; u;lh hkq CPU wi, msysgd we;s l=vd" wêfõ.S u;l j¾.hls' th ks;r m%fõY jk o;a;
    iy blauka m%fõYh i|yd Wmfoia ;djld,slj .nvd lrhs' flfia fj;;a" ksys; u;lh kYH fõ"
    thska woyia jkafka moaO;sh l%shd úrys; l< úg tys .nvd lr we;s ish¨u o;a; ke;s ù hk njhs'
    tneúka" ksys; u;lh ksjerÈj kYH f,i y÷kdf.k we;'

    Statement / m%ldYh D)
    Flash memory, used in USB drives and SSDs, is non-volatile. It retains data even without a power supply,
    making it ideal for portable and long-term storage. Unlike volatile memory, flash memory can maintain
    stored information indefinitely. Thus, flash is not volatile memory.
    USB Odjl iy SSD j, Ndú;d jk iefk,s u;lh kYH fkdfõ' n, iemhqula fkdue;sj jqjo th
    o;a; r|jd ;nd .kakd w;r" th wf;a f.k hd yels iy È.= ld,Sk .nvd lsÍu i|yd jvd;a iqÿiq
    fõ' kYH u;lh fuka fkdj" iefk,s u;lhg .nvd lr we;s f;dr;=re Èk kshuhla fkdue;sj mj;ajd
    .; yelsh' fï wkqj" iefk,s u;lh hkq kYH u;lh fkdfõ'

    Statement / m%ldYh E)
    RAM is the most common form of volatile memory in a computer system. It stores active data and
    instructions while the computer is on, but loses all content when the power is turned off. RAM plays a key
    role in system performance, but it is not permanent storage. Hence, RAM is a clear example of volatile
    memory.
    RAM hkq mß.Kl moaO;shl kYH u;lfha jvd;a fmdÿ wdldrhhs' mß.Klh l%shd;aul jk úg th
    l%shdldÍ o;a; iy Wmfoia .nvd lrhs" kuq;a úÿ,sh úikaê l< úg ish¨u wka;¾.;hka ke;s fõ'
    RAM moaO;s l%shdldß;ajfha m%Odk ld¾hNdrhla bgq lrhs" kuq;a th iaÓr .nvdjla fkdfõ' tneúka"
    RAM hkq kYH u;lh i|yd meyeÈ,s WodyrKhls'

    Therefore, the correct answer is 4) C and E only.
    tneúka" ksjerÈ ms<s;=r 4) C iy E muKs'

      2012 CONCEPT OF COMPUTER MCQ MARKING

   1. Who is considered as the first computer programmer?
      m%:u mß.Kl l%uf,aLlhd (computer programmer) f,i ie,flkafka ljqreka o?

          1)    John Von Neumann / fcdaka fjdka kshqudka
          2)    Blaise Pascal / íf,aia meial,a
          3)    Charles Babbage / pd,aia nefíÊ
          4)    Ada Augesta Lovelace / tavd T.iagd ,õf,aia
          5)    John Presper Eckert / fcdaka fm%iam¾ tl¾Ü

                                                                                                                                                         ( 2012 – 01 )
          Answer : 4 )
          Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
          Option / úl,amh 1)
          John Von Neumann was a brilliant mathematician and physicist who contributed significantly to the design
          of modern computer architecture. The "Von Neumann Architecture" is still the foundation for most
          computer systems today. However, he is not considered the first computer programmer. His work came
          much later than the early developments in computing.
          fcdaka fjdka kshquka hkq kQ;k mß.Kl jHqyfha ks¾udKhg ie,lsh hq;= odhl;ajhla ,nd ÿka olaI
          .Ks;{fhl= iy fN!;sl úoHd{fhls' fjdka kshquka jHqyh wog;a fndfyda mß.Kl moaO;s i|yd
          mokuhs' flfia fj;;a" Tyq m<uq mß.Kl l%uf,aLlhd f,i fkdie,fla' Tyqf.a ld¾hh
          mß.KllrKfha uq,a j¾Okhkag jvd fndfyda l,lg miqj isÿ úh'

          Option / úl,amh 2)
          Blaise Pascal was a French mathematician and philosopher who invented the Pascaline, one of the first
          mechanical calculators. Although he made early contributions to computing, he did not write or design
          any form of computer programs. His work focused more on hardware than software.
          íf,aia meial,a hkq m<uq hdka;sl

          intended for implementation on Charles Babbage’s Analytical Engine. Her notes on the machine,
          particularly "Note G," contained what is now recognized as the first published algorithm.

 2. Which of the following technologies has been used in the Third Generation Computers?
    ;=kajk mrïmrdfõ mß.Klj, Ndú; lrk ,oafoa my; oelafjk l=uk ;dlaIKh o?
    1)   Integrated Circuits / wkql,s; mßm: (ICs)
    2)   Large Scale Integration / úYd, mßudKfha wkql,s; (LSI)
    3)   Micro Processors / laIqø iliq
    4)   Transistors / g%dkaisiagr
    5)   Vacuum Tubes / ßla;l k<

                                                                                                ( 2012 – 02 )
    Answer : 1 )
    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    Option / úl,amh 1)
    Integrated Circuits (ICs) were the defining technology of third-generation computers, which emerged in
    the mid-1960s. These circuits allowed many transistors to be packed into a small chip, increasing
    processing power while reducing size and energy consumption. This made computers more efficient,
    reliable, and affordable.
    tAldnoaO mßm: (IC) hkq 1960 .Kkaj, ueo Nd.fha§ u;= jQ ;=kajk mrïmrdfõ mß.Klj, uQ,sl
    ;dlaIKhhs' fuu mßm: uÕska fndfyda g%dkaisiagr l=vd Ñmhlg weiqreï lsÍug bv ie,iqKq w;r"
    m%udKh iy n,Yla;s mßfNdackh wvq lrñka ieliqï n,h jeä lf<ah' fuh mß.Kl jvd;a
    ld¾hlaIu" úYajdiodhl iy oeßh yels ñ,lg ,nd ÿkafkah'

    Option / úl,amh 2)
    Large Scale Integration (LSI) technology involves placing thousands of transistors on a single chip. This
    technology became popular during the fourth generation of computers, which started around the mid-
    1970s. While it represents a major advancement in circuit design, it was not part of the third generation.
    uyd mßudK tAldnoaO lsÍfï (LSI) ;dlaIKhg g%dkaisiagr oyia .Kkla ;ks Ñmhla u; ;eîu we;=<;a
    fõ' 1970 .Kkaj, ueo Nd.fha§ muK wdrïN jQ mß.Klj, isõjk mrïmrdfõ§ fuu ;dlaIKh
    ckm%sh úh' th mßm: ks¾udKfha m%Odk ÈhqKqjla ksfhdackh l<o" th ;=kajk mrïmrdfõ fldgila
    fkdùh'

    Option / úl,amh 3)
    Microprocessors marked a significant breakthrough in computing and are the key feature of fourth-
    generation computers. They integrate entire CPU onto a single chip and were introduced in the early 1970s.
    laIqø ilikhka mß.KllrKfha ie,lsh hq;= bÈß .ukla iksgqyka l< w;r isõjk mrïmrdfõ
    mß.Klj, m%Odk ,laIKh fõ' tAjd iïmQ¾K CPU tl ;ks Ñmhlg tAldnoaO lrk w;r 1970
    .Kkaj, uq,a Nd.fha§ y÷kajd fok ,§'

    Option / úl,amh 4)
    Transistors were used in the second generation of computers, replacing vacuum tubes. While they were
    smaller and more efficient than vacuum tubes, they still required extensive wiring and manual assembly.
    Integrated Circuits eventually replaced them in the third generation. So, although transistors played an
    important historical role, they do not belong to the third generation.
    ßla; k, fjkqjg g%dkaisiagr fojk mrïmrdfõ mß.Klj, Ndú;d lrk ,§' tAjd ßla;l k, j,g
    jvd l=vd yd ld¾hlaIu jqjo" tAjdg ;ju;a mq¿,a /yeka we§u iy w;ska tl,ia lsÍu wjYH úh'

    ft;sydisl ld¾hNdrhla bgq l<o" tAjd ;=kajk mrïmrdjg wh;a fkdfõ'

    Option / úl,amh 5)
    Vacuum tubes were used in the very first generation of computers, such as the ENIAC and UNIVAC I.
    They were large, generated a lot of heat, and were prone to frequent failure. They were completely replaced
    by transistors in the second generation.
    ENIAC iy UNIVAC I jeks m<uq mrïmrdfõ mß.Klj, ßla; k, Ndú;d lrk ,§' tAjd úYd,
    úh" úYd, ;dmhla ckkh l< w;r ks;r ks;r wid¾:l ùug bv ;snqKs' fojk mrïmrdfõ§ tAjd
    iïmQ¾Kfhkau g%dkaisiagr u.ska m%;sia:dmkh úh'

 3. “The data in …… is read by using the Laser technology.”
    “…… ys we;s o;a; f,ai¾ ;dlaIKh Ndú; fldg lshjkq ,efí'”
    Which of the following is most appropriate to fill the blank in the above statement?
    by; m%ldYfha ysia;ek msrùu i|yd my; olafjk ljrla jvd;a iqÿiq fõ o?

    1)   Floppy Disk / kuH äialh
    2)   Magnetic Tape / pqïnls; mgh
    3)   Compact Disk / ixhqla; äialh
    4)   Magnetic Hard Disk / pqïnls; oDV äialh
    5)   Flash Memory / iefk<s u;lh

                                                                                                   ( 2012 – 03 )
    Answer : 3 )
    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    Option / úl,amh 1)
    A floppy disk is a magnetic storage medium that stores data using magnetic patterns. It is read and written
    using a magnetic read/write head, not laser technology. Floppy disks were widely used in the late 20th
    century but are now obsolete.
    kuH ;eáhla hkq pqïNl rgd Ndú;fhka o;a; .nvd lrk pqïNl .nvd udOHhls' th f,ai¾ ;dlaIKh
    fkdj pqïNl lshùfï$,sùfï ysila Ndú;fhka lshjd ,shkq ,efí' kuH ;eá 20 jk ishjfia w.Nd.fha§
    nyq,j Ndú;d jQ kuq;a oeka h,a mek f.dia we;'
    Option / úl,amh 2)
    Magnetic tapes are used mainly for backup and archival storage, and they also rely on magnetic heads to
    read and write data. They are slow but offer large storage capacity for sequential access. However, they
    do not use laser technology in any form of operation.
    pqïNl má m%Odk jYfhka Wmia: iy f,aLkd.dr .nvd lsÍu i|yd Ndú;d lrk w;r" o;a; lshùug

    Odß;djla ,nd fohs' flfia fj;;a" tAjd lsisÿ wdldrhl l%shdldß;ajhl§ f,ai¾ ;dlaIKh Ndú;d
    fkdlrhs'
    Option / úl,amh 3)
    Compact Disks (CDs) store data optically and are read by using laser technology. A laser beam scans the
    surface of the disk to detect pits and lands, which represent binary data. This technology allows for precise
    reading without physical contact.
    ixhqla; ;eá (CD) o;a; oDYHuh jYfhka .nvd lrk w;r f,ai¾ ;dlaIKh Ndú;fhka lshjkq
    ,efí' oaúuh o;a; ksfhdackh lrk j,j,a iy ìï y÷kd .ekSu i|yd f,ai¾ loïNhla ;eáfha

    Option / úl,amh 4)
    Hard disks use magnetic storage techniques and a read/write head that hovers close to spinning platters
    coated with magnetic material. They do not use lasers in their operation. Although hard disks are high-
    capacity and fast storage devices, they fall under magnetic technology, making this answer incorrect.
    oDV ;eá pqïNl .nvd Ys,amSh l%u iy pqïNl øjHj,ska wdf,am lr we;s lerflk ;eá wi, ießirk
    lshùfï$,sùfï ysila Ndú;d lrhs' Tjqka ;u l%shdldß;ajfha§ f,ai¾ Ndú;d fkdlrhs' oDV ;eá by<

    jerÈ lrhs'

    Since it operates electronically and not optically, this is not the correct answer.

 4. The component that decodes the instructions fetched into the CPU is called the
    CPU ;=<g wdyrK (fetch) lrk ,o Wmfoia úfla;kh (decode) lrkq ,nk ixrplh y÷kajkq
    ,nkafka

    1) Primary Memory / m%d:ñl u;lh (Primary Memory) kñks'
    2) Register Unit / frmsia;r tallh (Register Unit) kñks'
    3) Control Unit / md,k tallh (Control Unit) kñks'
    4) ALU / ALU kñks'
    5) Program Counter / l%uf,aL .Klh (Program Counter) kñks'

                                                                                                ( 2012 – 28 )
    Answer : 3 )
    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    When a CPU executes a program, it performs the fetch-decode-execute cycle
    CPU tlla jevigykla l%shd;aul lrk úg" th fijqï-úfla;k-bIaG pl%h isÿ lrhs

    •    Fetch: Get the instruction from memory / fiùu( u;lfhka Wmfoia ,nd .kak'
    •    Decode: Understand what the instruction means / úfla;kh( Wmfoia j, f;areu f;areï .kak'
    •    Execute: Performing the task (ex: add numbers, move data, etc.).
         bIaG lsÍu( ld¾hh bgq lsÍu ^Wod( wxl tl;= lsÍu" o;a; f.khdu wdÈh&'

    The decode step involves interpreting the binary instruction (opcode + operands) and determining the
    operation to perform. It is done by the control unit.
    Decode mshjrg oaúuh Wmfoia (opcode + operands) w¾: ksrEmKh lsÍu iy isÿ l< hq;=
    fufyhqu ;SrKh lsÍu we;=<;a fõ' th md,k tAllh úiska isÿ lrkq ,efí'

    The control unit (CU) directs the operation of the processor, decodes the instructions fetched into the
    CPU and sends control signals to the ALU, memory, and I/O devices to execute the instruction.
    md,k tAllh ^CU& ilikfha l%shdldß;ajh fufyhjhs" CPU fj; ,nd .;a Wmfoia úfla;kh lrhs
    iy Wmfoia l%shd;aul lsÍu i|yd ALU" u;lh iy I/O Wmdx. fj; md,k ix{d hjhs'

    Control Unit is the brain inside the brain (CPU) that understands what needs to be done.
    md,k tAllh hkq fud<h ^CPU& ;=< we;s fud<h jk w;r th l< hq;= foa f;areï .kS'

    Therefore, option 3) is the correct answer.
    tneúka" 3) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'

    Why the other options are incorrect / wfkl=;a úl,am jerÈ jkafka wehs
    Option/ úl,amh 1)
    Primary memory stores data and program instructions. RAM is a type of primary memory. It does not
    interpret or decode anything, just stores it.
    m%d:ñl u;lh o;a; iy jevigyka Wmfoia .nvd lrhs' RAM hkq m%d:ñl u;l j¾.hls' th
    lsisjla w¾: ksrEmKh lrkafka fyda úfla;kh lrkafka ke;" th .nvd lrhs'

    Option/ úl,amh 2)
    Registers are small, fast memory locations inside the CPU (like ACC, IR, etc.). They are used for
    temporarily holding data and instructions.
    frðia;r hkq CPU tl ;=< we;s l=vd" fõ.j;a u;l ia:dk fõ ^ACC, IR, wdÈh&' tAjd o;a; iy
    Wmfoia ;djld,slj r|jd ;nd .ekSu i|yd Ndú;d lrhs'

    Option/ úl,amh 4)
    Executes arithmetic and logical operations (ex: ADD, SUBTRACT, AND, OR). It waits for instructions
    from the control unit to act.
    wxl .Ks;uh iy ;d¾lsl fufyhqï l%shd;aul lrhs ^Wod( ADD, SUBTRACT, AND, OR& th md,k
    tAllfhka Wmfoia l%shd;aul jk f;la n,d isà'

    Option/ úl,amh 5)

 5. Which of the following data storage devices provides the fastest random access?
    my; i|yka wdphk Wmdx. w;=frka fõ.j;au iiïNdù m%fõYh (Random Access) imhkq ,nkafka
    l=ulska o?

    1)   Main memory / m%Odk u;lh
    2)   Magnetic disk / pqqïnls; äialh
    3)   CD/DVD
    4)   Flash drive / iefk<s u;l Wmdx.
    5)   Hard disk / oDV äialh

                                                                                                   ( 2012 – 29 )
    Answer : 1 )
    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    Option/ úl,amh 1)
    Main memory, also known as RAM (Random Access Memory), is specifically designed to support
    extremely fast random-access operations. It allows the processor to directly and quickly access any
    memory cell without needing to go through a sequence of locations. RAM is volatile and temporarily
    stores data for currently running programs.
    m%Odk u;lh" RAM (iiïNdù m%fõY u;lh& f,io ye¢kafõ" w;sYh fõ.j;a wyUq-m%fõY fufyhqï
    i|yd iydh ùu i|yd úfYaIfhka ks¾udKh lr we;' th ilikhg ia:dk wkqms<sfj,la yryd
    hdulska f;drj ´kEu u;l ffi,hlg iDcqj iy blaukska m%fõY ùug bv i,ihs' RAM kIH jk
    w;r oekg l%shd;aul jk jevigyka i|yd o;a; ;djld,slj .nvd lrhs'

    Option/ úl,amh 2)
    Magnetic disks, such as floppy disks or older external drives, use a magnetic surface to store data.
    Accessing data randomly on a magnetic disk is much slower due to the mechanical movement of the
    read/write head. It must physically spin and position the head over the correct track, which increases access
    time. Thus, magnetic disks are significantly slower than main memory for random access.
    kuH ;eá fyda merKs ndysr Odjl jeks pqïnl ;eá" o;a; .nvd lsÍu i|yd pqïnl u;=msgla Ndú;d
    lrhs' lshùfï$,sùfï ysfiys hdka;%sl p,kh fya;=fjka pqïnl ;eáhl iiïNdù o;a; m%fõY lsÍu
    fndfyda fihska ukao.dó fõ' th fN!;slj N%uKh ù ysi ksjerÈ Odjk m:h u; ia:dk.; l< hq;=
    w;r tuÕska m%fõY ld,h jeä fõ' fï wkqj" pqïnl ;eá iiïNdù m%fõYh i|yd m%Odk u;lhg
    jvd ie,lsh hq;= f,i ukao.dó fõ'

    Option/ úl,amh 3)
    CDs and DVDs are optical storage devices that read data using a laser. Like magnetic disks, they involve
    mechanical rotation and are sequential in nature, which makes random access comparatively slow. The
    laser must locate specific tracks and sectors, and the latency involved makes them unsuitable for fast
    random-access operations.

RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                               -11-                                         #ictfromabc

    CD iy DVD hkq f,ai¾ Ndú;fhka o;a; lshjk oDYH .nvd Wmdx. fõ' pqïnl ;eá fuka" tAjd
    hdka;%sl N%uKh we;=<;a jk w;r wkql%ñl iajNdjhla .kakd w;r tuÕska iiïNdù m%fõYh idfmalaIj
    ukao.dó fõ' f,ai¾ ksYaÑ; Odjk m: iy wxY fidhd .; hq;= w;r" Bg iïnkaO m%udoh tAjd
    fõ.j;a iiïNdù-m%fõY fufyhqï i|yd kqiqÿiq lrhs'

    Option/ úl,amh 4)
    Flash drives are faster than traditional magnetic or optical storage and allow faster access times with no
    moving parts. They offer decent random-access performance, especially compared to CDs or hard disks.
    However, they are still slower than RAM and not designed to match the speed of main memory during
    high-performance computing tasks.
    iefk,s u;l Wmdx. idïm%odhsl pqïnl fyda oDYH .nvdjg jvd fõ.j;a jk w;r p,kh jk fldgia
    fkdue;sj fõ.j;a m%fõY fõ,djka ,nd fohs' tAjd CD fyda oDV ;eá yd ii|k úg fyd| iiïNdù
    m%fõY ld¾h idOkhla ,nd fohs' flfia fj;;a" tAjd ;ju;a RAM j,g jvd ukao.dó jk w;r by<
    ld¾hidOkhla iys; mß.Kl ld¾hhka w;r;=r m%Odk u;lfha fõ.hg .e,fmk mßÈ ks¾udKh lr
    fkdue;'

    Option/ úl,amh 5)
    Hard disks use spinning platters and a mechanical arm to read and write data magnetically. Although they
    provide large storage capacities, their random-access speed is much slower due to mechanical delays. Like
    other magnetic media, the head needs to move to the correct position before accessing data. As a result,
    hard disks are far slower than main memory for random access.
    oDV ;eá pqïNlj o;a; lshùug iy ,sùug N%uKh jk ;eá iy hdka;sl                  % w;la Ndú;d lrhs' tAjd
    úYd, .nvd Odß;djla ,nd ÿkako" hdka;sl       % m%udohka fya;=fjka tAjdfha iiïNdù m%fõY fõ.h fnfyúka
    ukao.dó fõ' wfkl=;a pqïNl udOH fuka" o;a; fj; m%fõY ùug fmr ysi ksjerÈ ia:dkhg .uka
    l< hq;=h' tys m%;sM,hla f,i" iiïNdù m%fõYh i|yd oDV ;eá m%Odk u;lhg jvd fnfyúka
    ukao.dó fõ'

    Therefore, option 1) is the correct answer.
    tneúka" 1) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'

      2013 CONCEPT OF COMPUTER MCQ MARKING

   1. A high speed temporary storage, which is a part of the microprocessor that holds data and
      instructions during the execution, is called ……
      l%shdlrùu (execution) w;r;=r § o;a; yd Wmfoia ord .ksñka" laIqø ilikfha (microprocessor)
      fldgila                       f,i mj;sk" wêfõ.S ;djld,sl wdphkhla (high speed temporary storage)
      …… f,i ye¢kafõ' by; ysia;ek msrùu i|yd jvd;a u fhda.H ms<s;=r l=ula o?

          1) Registers                  2) RAM                      3) Virtual Memory                        4) EPROM                   5) Flash Memory
             frðia;r                                                   w;;H u;lh                                                           iefk,s u;lh

                                                                                                                                                         ( 2013 – 03 )
          Answer : 1 )
          Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
          Option / úl,amh 1)
          Registers are small, extremely fast memory locations located directly inside the CPU (microprocessor).
          They temporarily hold data, instructions, and addresses currently being used during the execution of
          programs. Since they are physically part of the processor, access time is extremely low, making them the
          fastest form of memory.
          frðiag¾ hkq ^CPU& laIqø ilikh ;=< iDcqju msysgd we;s l=vd" w;sYh fõ.j;a u;l ia:dk fõ'

          RAM (Random Access Memory) is used to store data and programs that are currently running, but it is
          not a part of the microprocessor itself. RAM is slower than registers and exists externally to the CPU. It

 2. Microprocessors are usually compared by their clock speed, measured in …… or by their
    word size, measured in …… that can be processed in a single clock cycle. Which of
    the following is most appropriate to fill the blanks in the above statement?
    laIqø ilik idudkHfhka ii|kq ,nkafka …… j,ska ukskq ,nk >áld fõ.fhka
    (clock speed) fyda tal >áld pl%hl § (single clock cycle) ilid .; yels mofha m%udKh (word size)

     1)   Bits, Megahertz / ìgq" fu.dy¾Üia
     2)   Bytes, Gigahertz / nhsg" .s.dy¾Üia
     3)   Gigahertz, Bytes / .s.dy¾Üia" nhsg
     4)   Megahertz, Bits / fu.dy¾Üia" ìg
     5)   Seconds, Bits / ;;amr" ìgq

                                                                                                    ( 2013 – 04 )
     Answer : 4 )
     Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
     Option / úl,amh 1)
     Clock speed, which describes how many cycles a processor completes per second, is measured in
     frequency units like Hertz, Megahertz, or Gigahertz. Bits, on the other hand, are the fundamental units for
     data and word size, indicating how much data can be processed at once. Therefore, the order of units is
     reversed and mismatched for the respective blanks.
     ilikhla ;;amrhlg fldmuK pl% iïmQ¾K lrkjdo hkak úia;r lrk >áld fõ.h" y¾Üia"
     fu.dy¾Üia fyda .s.dy¾Üia jeks ixLHd; tAll j,ska ukskq ,efí' wfkla w;g" ìgq hkq o;a; iy
     jpk m%udKh i|yd uQ,sl tAll jk w;r" tljr fldmuK o;a; ielish yelso hkak olajhs' tneúka"
     tAll wkqms<sfj, m%;sf,dau lr wod< ysia ;eka i|yd fkd.e,fma'

     Option / úl,amh 2)
     "Bytes" represent a measure of data quantity, specifically 8 bits, and while related to word size, they are
     not the primary measure of clock speed. "Gigahertz" is a correct unit for clock speed, but it's placed in the
     second blank where word size should be.
     nhsÜ hkq o;a; m%udKfha ñkqula ksfhdackh lrhs" úfYaIfhka ìgq 8 la jk w;r" jpk m%udKhg wod<
     jqjo" tAjd >áld fõ.fha m%d:ñl ñkqu fkdfõ' Gigahertz hkq Trf,daiq fõ.h i|yd ksjerÈ tAllhls"
     kuq;a th jpk m%udKh ;sìh hq;= fojk ysia ;ek ;nd we;'

     Option / úl,amh 3)
     This option identifies "Gigahertz" as a measure of clock speed, which is a common unit for modern
     microprocessors, indicating billions of cycles per second. However, it uses "Bytes" for word size. While
     64-bit processor processes 8 bytes at a time, "Bits" is the direct and fundamental unit used to describe a
     processor's word size. Therefore, while plausible, it's not as precise for "word size" as "Bits."
     fuu úl,amh Gigahertz >áld fõ.fha ñkqula f,i y÷kd .kS" th kùk laIqø ilik i|yd fmdÿ
     tAllhla jk w;r th ;;amrhlg ì,shk .Kkl pl% olajhs' flfia fj;;a" th jpk m%udKh i|yd

    Option / úl,amh 4)
    "Megahertz" is a widely recognized unit for measuring clock speed, representing millions of cycles per
    second, and applies to a broad range of microprocessors, from older to some modern ones. "Bits" is the
    precise and fundamental unit for measuring word size, which defines how many bits of data a processor
    can handle in a single operation. This pairing accurately describes the two key comparison metrics
    mentioned.
    Megahertz hkq >áld fõ.h uekSu i|yd mq¿,a f,i ms<s.;a tAllhla jk w;r th ;;amrhlg pl%

    fuu hq.,kh i|yka lr we;s m%Odk ixikaokd;aul ñkqï fol ksjerÈj úia;r lrhs'

    Option / úl,amh 5)
    "Seconds" measure time duration, not frequency or clock speed. Clock speed is measured in Hertz, not
    just seconds. While "Bits" is correct for word size, the incorrect unit for clock speed makes this option
    unsuitable. Therefore, it fails to accurately represent how microprocessors are typically compared using
    clock speed.
    ;;amr ukskq ,nkafka ld, iSudj ñi ixLHd;h fyda >áld fõ.h fkdfõ' >áld fõ.h ;;amr j,ska
    muKla fkdj y¾Üia j,ska ukskq ,efí' Bits jpk m%udKh i|yd ksjerÈ jqjo" Trf,daiq fõ.h i|yd

 3. Typically the cache memory is used to store ……
    ksys; u;lh(cache memory) idudkHfhka Ndú; lrkq ,nkafka …… .nvd lsÍugh'
    by; ysia;ek msrùu i|yd jvd;a u fhda.H ms<s;=r l=ula o?
    1) a large volume of data temporarily
       úYd, o;a; mßudjla ;djld,slj
    2) the least frequently accessed data permanently
       wju ksrka;rfhka (least frequently) m%fõY lrkq ,nk o;a; iaÓrj
    3) the least frequently accessed data temporarily.
       wju ksrka;rfhka (least frequently) m%fõY lrkq ,nk o;a; ;djld,slj
    4) the most frequently accessed data temporarily.
       jeäu ksrka;rfhka (most frequently) m%fõY lrkq ,nk o;a; ;djld,slj
    5) the most frequently accessed data permanently.
       jeäu ksrka;rfhka (most frequently) m%fõY lrkq ,nk o;a; iaÓrj

                                                                                                ( 2013 – 05 )
    Answer : 4 )

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu

    Option / úl,amh 1)
    Cache memory is not designed for large volume storage. It is a very limited and high-speed memory used
    for storing a small set of frequently accessed data. Large volumes of temporary data are better suited for
    RAM, not cache.
    úYd, mßudKfha .nvd lsÍu i|yd ksys; u;lh ks¾udKh lr ke;' th ks;r m%fõY jk l=vd o;a;
    lÜg,hla .nvd lsÍu i|yd Ndú;d lrk b;d iSñ; iy wêfõ.S u;lhls' ;djld,sl o;a; úYd,
    mßudKfha RAM i|yd jvd;a iqÿiq fõ" ksys; u;lh fkdfõ'

RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                               -15-                                          #ictfromabc

    Option / úl,amh 2)
    Cache memory stores frequently used data, not rarely accessed data. Also, cache memory is temporary,
    not permanent. Data in cache can be replaced or cleared at any moment, especially when newer, frequently
    used data needs to be stored.
    ksys; u;lh ks;r Ndú;d jk o;a; .nvd lrhs" l,d;=rlska m%fõY jk o;a; fkdfõ' tfiau" ksys;
    u;lh ;djld,slhs" iaÓr fkdfõ' úfYaIfhka kj ks;r Ndú;d jk o;a; .nvd lsÍug wjYH jQ úg"
    ´kEu fudfyd;l ksys; u;lfha o;a; m%;sia:dmkh lsÍug fyda ysia lsÍug yelsh'

    Option / úl,amh 3)
    Cache memory prioritizes the most frequently accessed data. There is no benefit to storing rarely used data
    in the fastest and smallest memory location — it's inefficient and against the very purpose of cache.
    ksys; u;lh ks;r m%fõY jk o;a; j,g m%uqL;djh fohs' l,d;=rlska Ndú;d jk o;a; fõ.j;au
    iy l=vdu u;l ia:dkfha .nvd lsÍfuka lsisÿ m%;s,dNhla fkdue; - th wld¾hlaIu jk w;r ksys;
    u;lfha wruqKgu mgyeksh'

    Option / úl,amh 4)
    Cache memory is used to store data and instructions that the processor needs most often, and it keeps them
    temporarily so the CPU doesn't have to fetch them repeatedly from slower memory (like RAM or hard
    drive). Since cache memory is expensive and limited in size, it focuses on storing only the most frequently
    used content to boost performance. When the CPU accesses the same data multiple times, the cache
    ensures it can do so quickly.
    ilikhg fndfyda úg wjYH jk o;a; iy Wmfoia .nvd lsÍug ksys; u;lh Ndú;d lrk w;r"
    th tAjd ;djld,slj ;nd .kS" túg CPU yg ukao.dó u;lfhka ^RAM fyda oDV ;eáh jeks& kej;
    kej; ,nd .ekSug isÿ fkdfõ' ksys; u;lh ñ, wêl jk w;r m%udKfhka iSñ; neúka" ld¾h
    idOkh jeä ÈhqKq lsÍu i|yd th ks;r Ndú;d jk wka;¾.;h muKla .nvd lsÍu flfrys wjOdkh
    fhduq lrhs' CPU tlu o;a; lsysm j;djla m%fõY jk úg" ksys; u;lh th blaukska l< yels nj
    iy;sl lrhs'

    Option / úl,amh 5)
    Cache memory prioritizes the most frequently accessed data. There is no benefit to storing rarely used data
    in the fastest and smallest memory location — it's inefficient and against the very purpose of cache.
    ksys; u;lh ks;r m%fõY jk o;a; j,g m%uqL;djh fohs' l,d;=rlska Ndú;d jk o;a; fõ.j;au
    iy l=vdu u;l ia:dkfha .nvd lsÍfuka lsisÿ m%;s,dNhla fkdue; - th wld¾hlaIu jk w;r ksys;
    u;lfha wruqKgu mgyeksh'

 4. Babbage’s Difference Engine is based on ……
    nefíÊf.a “Difference hka;%h” mdol ù we;af;a …… u; h' by; ysia;ek msrùu
    i|yd jvd;a u fhda.H ms<s;=r l=ula o?

    1)   mechanical technology / hdka;%sl ;dlaIKh
    2)   vacuum tube technology / ßla;l k, ;dlaIKh
    3)   transistor technology / g%dkaisiagr ;dlaIKh
    4)   Integrated Circuit (IC) technology / wkql,s; mßm: (IC) ;dlaIKh
    5)   Very Large Scale Integrated (VLSI) Circuit technology.
         b;d úYd, mßudKfha wkql,s; mßm: (VLSI) ;dlaIKh

                                                                                                  ( 2013 – 07 )
    Answer : 1 )
    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    Option / úl,amh 1)
    The Difference Engine, designed by Charles Babbage in the early 19th century, was a purely mechanical
    device. It used gears, levers, and cogs to perform arithmetic calculations — specifically polynomial
    evaluations using the method of finite differences. There were no electronic components, as this machine
    predates electricity-based computing. It was an engineering marvel of its time and laid the foundation for
    future computing.
    19 jk ishjfia uq,a Nd.fha§ pd,aia nefíÊ úiska ks¾udKh lrk ,o ä*rkaia tkaðu iïmQ¾Kfhkau
    hdka;%sl WmlrKhls' th wxl .Ks;uh .Kkh lsÍï isÿ lsÍu i|yd .sh¾" ,Sjr iy oe;s frdao
    Ndú;d lf<ah - úfYaIfhka iSñ; fjkialï l%uh Ndú;d lrñka nyqmo we.hSï' fuu hka;%h úÿ,sh
    u; mokï jQ mß.KllrKhg fmr mej;s neúka bf,lafg%dksl ixrpl fkd;snqKs' th tys ld,fha
    bxðfkare wdYap¾hhla jQ w;r wkd.; mß.KllrKh i|yd wä;d,u oeóh'
    Option / úl,amh 2)
    Vacuum tube technology came much later, in the 1940s, and was used in first-generation electronic
    computers like the ENIAC. Babbage lived in the 1800s, long before vacuum tubes were invented. So, this
    is chronologically incorrect for the Difference Engine.
    ßla; k, ;dlaIKh fndfyda l,lg miqj - 1940 .Kkaj, meñKs w;r ENIAC jeks m<uq mrïmrdfõ
    bf,lafg%dksl mß.Klj, Ndú;d lrk ,§' ßla; k, fidhd .ekSug fndfyda l,lg fmr" 1800
    .Kkaj, nefíÊ Ôj;a úh' tneúka" fuh ä*rkaia tkaðu i|yd ld,dkql%ñlj jerÈh'
    Option / úl,amh 3)
    Transistors replaced vacuum tubes in the second generation of computers starting in the 1950s. They
    enabled smaller, more reliable computers — but again, this is over 100 years after Babbage's work. The
    Difference Engine was entirely non-electronic, making this choice invalid.
    1950 .Kkaj, wdrïN jk fojk mrïmrdfõ mß.Klj, g%dkaisiagr ßla; k, m%;sia:dmkh lf<ah'
    Tjqka l=vd" jvd úYajdiodhl mß.Kl il%Sh l<y - kuq;a kej;;a" fuh nefíÊf.a ld¾hfhka jir
    100 lg jvd jeä ld,hlg miqjh' ä*rkaia tkaðu iïmQ¾Kfhkau bf,lafg%dksl fkdjk neúka fuu
    f;aÍu jerÈh'
    Option / úl,amh 4)
    IC technology was developed in the 1960s, allowing thousands of transistors to be placed on a single chip.
    This was a major step in modern computing, but it is far removed from the mechanical age in which
    Babbage worked. His machine didn't use electronics at all.
    IC ;dlaIKh 1960 .Kkaj, ixj¾Okh lrk ,o w;r" g%dkaisiagr oyia .Kkla ;ks Ñmhla u;
    ;eîug bv i,ihs' fuh kQ;k mß.KllrKfha m%Odk mshjrla jQ kuq;a th nefíÊ jev l< hdka;%sl
    hq.fhka fndfyda ÿria h' Tyqf.a hka;%h lsisfia;au bf,lafg%dksl WmlrK Ndú;d lf<a ke;'
    Option / úl,amh 5)
    VLSI technology, developed in the 1970s and 1980s, is used in today’s microprocessors with millions of
    transistors. This is a modern digital technology, completely irrelevant to the historical, mechanical design
    of the Difference Engine.
    1970 iy 1980 .Kkaj, ixj¾Okh lrk ,o VLSI ;dlaIKh" ñ,shk .Kkla g%dkaisiagr iys; wo
    laIqø ilikj, Ndú;d fõ' fuh kùk äðg,a ;dlaIKhls" th ä*rkaia tkaðfï ft;sydisl" hdka;sl                       %
    ie,iqug iïmQ¾Kfhkau wod< fkdfõ'

 5. Which of the following components is located outside the microprocessor?
    laIqø ieliqï tallhg (Microprocessor) ndysrj msysgd we;af;a my; olajd we;s l=uk ixrplh o?
    1)   Arithmetic Logic Unit / wxl.Ks; ;¾l tallh (ALU)
    2)   RAM
    3)   Control Unit / md,k tallh
    4)   Registers / frðia;r
    5)   Level 1 cache memory / m<uq uÜgfï ksys; u;lh

                                                                                                   ( 2013 – 08 )
    Answer : 2 )
    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    Option / úl,amh 1)
    The ALU is an internal unit of the microprocessor. It performs arithmetic operations (like addition,
    subtraction) and logic operations (like AND, OR, NOT). It is one of the core functional blocks of the CPU
    and works directly with registers and the control unit.
    ALU hkq laIqø ilikfha wNHka;r tAllhls' th wxl .Ks;uh fufyhqï ^tl;= lsÍu" wvq lsÍu
    jeks& iy ;d¾lsl fufyhqï ^AND, OR, NOT jeks& isÿ lrhs' th CPU ys uQ,sl l%shdldÍ fldgia j,ska
    tlla jk w;r frðiag¾ iy md,k tAllh iuÕ iDcqju l%shd lrhs'

    Option / úl,amh 2)
    RAM is a separate physical component located outside the microprocessor on the motherboard. It serves
    as the main memory where data and programs are stored temporarily while being used by the CPU. The
    microprocessor accesses data from RAM through a system bus, but RAM is not embedded inside the CPU.
    It plays a vital role in system performance but is not part of the microprocessor architecture itself.
    RAM hkq ujq mqjrefõ laIqø ilikfhka msg; msysgd we;s fjku fN!;sl ixrplhls' th CPU úiska
    Ndú;d lrk w;r;=r o;a; iy jevigyka ;djld,slj .nvd lrk m%Odk u;lh f,i l%shd lrhs'
    laIqø ilikh RAM fj;ska moaO;s nia r:hla yryd o;a; fj; m%fõY fõ" kuq;a RAM CPU ;=<
    we;=<;a lr fkdue;' th moaO;s l%shdldß;ajfha jeo.;a ld¾hNdrhla bgq lrhs kuq;a laIqø ilik
    wdlD;s ks¾udK Ys,amfhau fldgila fkdfõ'

    Option / úl,amh 3)
    The control unit is also a core part of the microprocessor. It is responsible for directing operations within
    the CPU by decoding instructions and coordinating between the ALU, registers, and memory. It resides
    inside the CPU.
    md,k tAllh laIqø ilikfha uQ,sl fldgils' Wmfoia úfla;kh lsÍfuka iy ALU frðiag¾ iy
    u;lh w;r iïnkaëlrKh lsÍfuka CPU ;=< fufyhqï fufyhùu i|yd th j.lsj hq;=h' th
    CPU ;=< msysgd we;'

    Option / úl,amh 4)
    Registers are very small, high-speed memory units located inside the microprocessor. They temporarily
    store data, addresses, or instructions during execution. Registers are integral to the internal operation of
    the processor and directly interact with the ALU and control unit.
    frðiag¾ hkq laIqø ilikh ;=< msysgd we;s b;d l=vd" wêfõ.S u;l tAll fõ' tAjd l%shd;aul lsÍfï§
    o;a;" ,smsk fyda Wmfoia ;djld,slj .nvd lrhs' frðiag¾" ilikfha wNHka;r l%shdldß;ajhg
    w;HjYH jk w;r ALU iy md,k tAllh iuÕ iDcqju wka;¾ l%shd lrhs'

    Option / úl,amh 5)
    Level 1 (L1) cache is built into the microprocessor chip. It is the fastest cache memory and stores
    frequently accessed instructions and data for rapid retrieval. Although some caches (like L2 or L3) may
    be located outside the CPU core, L1 cache is internal.
    1 jk uÜgfï L1 ksys; u;lh laIqø ilik Ñmhg f.dvk.d we;' th fõ.j;au ksys; u;lh jk
    w;r fõ.j;a kej; ,nd .ekSu i|yd ks;r m%fõY jk Wmfoia iy o;a; .nvd lrhs' iuyr ksys;
    ^L2 fyda L3 jeks& CPU yrfhka msg; msysgd ;sìh yels jqjo" L1 ksys; u;lh wNHka;r fõ'

    Therefore, option 2) is the correct answer.
    tneúka" 2) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'

 6. Consider the following statements about Firmware:
    iaÓrdx. (firmware) ms<sn|j my; oelafjk j.ka;s i,lkak(

    A. Firmware is the program required to bootup a computer system.
       mß.Klhla l%shdlrùu wdrïN lsÍu (bootup) i|yd wjYH l%uf,aLh iaÓrdx.hla fõ'
    B. Firmware is incorporated in washing machines
       frÈ fidaok hka;%j, iaÓrdx. we;=<;a fõ'
    C. Firmware can be easily changed later on

    Which of the above statements is/are correct?

 3. Which of the following has the fastest access speed?

    Answer : 2 )
    The correct answer is Register Memory
    ksjerÈ ms<s;=r frðia;r u;lh fõ

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    In a computer system, memory hierarchy determines the speed and proximity of storage components to
    the CPU.
    mß.Kl moaO;shl" u;l OQrdj,sh CPU fj; .nvd ixrplj, fõ.h iy ióm;ajh ;SrKh lrhs'

    Here's the typical order from fastest to slowest
    fõ.j;au isg ukao.dóu olajd idudkH wkqms<sfj, my; oelafõ

    Register Memory / frðia;r u;lh
    Fastest and located inside the CPU. Used to store data that the CPU is currently processing.
    fõ.j;au iy CPU ;=< msysgd we;' CPU oekg ilik o;a; .nvd lsÍug Ndú;d lrhs'

    Cache Memory / ksys; u;lh
    Slightly slower than registers, but faster than main memory. Located close to the CPU.
    frðia;r j,g jvd ;rula ukao.dó kuq;a m%Odk u;lhg jvd fõ.j;a' CPU tlg wdikakj msysgd
    we;'

    Extended/Main Memory (RAM) / úialD; u;lh
    Slower than cache, holds active data and programs.
    cache g jvd ukao.dó kuq;a" l%shdldÍ o;a; iy jevigyka r|jd ;nd .kS'

    Flash Memory / iefk,s u;lh
    Used in SSDs, portable storage. Non-volatile but much slower than RAM or cache.
    SSD j, Ndú;d fõ" wf;a f.k hd yels .nvdj' kYH fkdjk kuq;a RAM fyda cache g jvd
    fndfyda ukao.dó'

    Virtual Memory / w;:HrEmS u;lh
    Part of storage (hard drive/SSD) used as "extra RAM". Slowest, since it involves disk access.
    w;sfral RAM f,i Ndú;d lrk .nvdfõ fldgils ^oDV ;eáh&' ;eá m%fõYh wjYH jk neúka"
    ukao.dó fõ'

    Therefore, option 2) is the correct answer.
    tneúka" 2) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'

 4. Consider the following statements on Static Random Access Memory (SRAM):
    iaÒ;sl iiïNdù m%fõY u;lh (SRAM) iïnkaOfhka my; olajd we;s m%ldY i,ld n,kak(
    A. SRAM needs periodic refreshing / SRAM i|yd ld,dj¾; mqnqÿ lsÍula (refreshing) wjYH fõ'
    B. It is used for Cache memory / th ksys; u;lh i|yd Ndú; fõ'
    C. Registers are made of SRAMs / frðia;r ksmojd we;af;a SRAM u.sks'
    Which of the above statement(s) is/are correct?
    by; j.ka;s w;=frka i;H jkafka l=ula o?

    1) A only.         2) B only.          3) A and B only.   4) A and C only.   5) B and C only.
       A muKs.            B muKs.             A yd B muKs.       A yd C muKs.       B yd C muKs.

                                                                                             ( 2014 – 43 )
    Answer : 5 )
    Evaluating each statement / tla tla m%ldYh meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    Statement / m%ldYh A)
    Incorrect. DRAM (Dynamic RAM) needs periodic refreshing. SRAM retains data as long as power is
    supplied, no refreshing needed.
    jerÈhs' DRAM ^.;sl RAM& i|yd jßka jr kejqï lsÍula wjYH fõ' n,h imhk ;dla l,a
    SRAM o;a; r|jd .kS" kejqï lsÍula wjYH fkdfõ'

    Statement / m%ldYh B)
    Correct. SRAM is faster and more expensive than DRAM. Therefore, it's used in cache memory (L1, L2,
    L3) in CPUs.
    ksjerÈhs' SRAM" DRAM j,g jvd fõ.j;a yd ñ, wêlhs' tneúka" th CPU j, ksys; u;lfha
    ^L1" L2" L3& Ndú;d fõ'

    Statement / m%ldYh C)
    Correct. Registers are built using flip-flops, which are often implemented using SRAM cells or similar
    static circuits. So, this is generally considered true.
    ksjerÈhs' frðia;r f.dvk.d we;af;a ms,s-fmd< Ndú;fhka jk w;r" tAjd fndfyda úg SRAM ffi,
    fyda tA yd iudk iaÓ;sl mßm: Ndú;fhka l%shd;aul fõ' tneúka" fuh idudkHfhka i;H f,i ie,fla'

    Therefore, the correct answer is 5) B and C only.
    tneúka" ksjerÈ ms<s;=r 5) B iy C muKs'

      pd,aia nefíÊj “mß.Klfha mshd” f,ig iuyre y÷kaj;s' thg fya;=j ù we;af;a Tyq"

          Answer : 4 )
          Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
          Option/ úl,amh 1)
          The Pascaline, one of the earliest mechanical calculators, was invented by Blaise Pascal, a French
          mathematician, in the 17th century. It was capable of performing basic addition and subtraction. Charles
          Babbage, although a pioneer in computing, had no involvement in the invention of the Pascaline. He
          worked on more complex mechanical computing machines like the Difference Engine and the Analytical
          Engine.
          merKs;u hdka;%sl .Kl hka;%j,ska tlla jk meial,hska" 17 jk ishjfia§ m%xY .Ks;{fhl= jk
          íf,aia meial,a úiska fidhd .kakd ,§' th uQ,sl tl;= lsÍu iy wvq lsÍu isÿ lsÍug iu;a úh'
          mß.KllrKfha mqfrda.dñfhl= jqjo pd,aia nefíÊ meial,hska fidhd .ekSug iïnkaO fkdùh' Tyq
          ä*rkaia tkaðu iy úYaf,aIK tkaðu jeks jvd;a ixlS¾K hdka;%sl mß.Kl hka;% u; jev lf<ah'

          Option/ úl,amh 2)
          The first re-programmable electronic computer is widely recognized as the ENIAC or, later, the EDVAC,
          which introduced the stored-program concept. These machines were developed long after Babbage’s time.
          Charles Babbage's Analytical Engine, while conceptually programmable using punched cards, was never
          completed and was entirely mechanical, not electronic. Therefore, he cannot be credited with inventing a
          reprogrammable electronic computing machine.
          m<uq kej; jevigyka.; l< yels bf,lafg%dksl mß.Klh ENIAC fyda miqj .nvd l< jevigyka
          ixl,amh y÷kajd ÿka EDVAC f,i mq¿,a f,i ms<s.efka' fuu hka;% nefíÊf.a ld,fhka fndfyda

          hdka;%sl úh - bf,lafg%dksl fkdfõ' tneúka" kej; jevigyka.; l< yels bf,lafg%dksl mß.Kl

    Option/ úl,amh 3)
    Charles Babbage lived in the 19th century and died in 1871, long before IBM or personal computers
    existed. The first personal computers were developed in the 1970s and 1980s, with IBM introducing its
    version in 1981. Therefore, Babbage could not have played any role in this development. This statement
    is anachronistic and historically inaccurate.
    pd,aia nefíÊ 19 jk ishjfia Ôj;a jQ w;r IBM fyda mqoa.,sl mß.Kl mej;Sug fndfyda l,lg fmr
    1871 § ñh .sfhah' m<uq mqoa.,sl mß.Kl 1970 iy 1980 .Kkaj, ixj¾Okh lrk ,o w;r" IBM
    1981 § ;ukaf.au wkqjdohla y÷kajd ÿkafkah' tneúka" fuu ixj¾Okh i|yd nefíÊg lsisÿ
    ld¾hNdrhla bgq l< fkdyels úh' fuu m%ldYh ld,dkqrEmS fkdjk w;r ft;sydisl jYfhka idjoHfõ'
    Option/ úl,amh 4)
    While Charles Babbage did not explicitly name the model as "Input, Process, Output", his design of the
    Analytical Engine included these fundamental components. The machine had an input method a
    processing unit, and an output mechanism. These ideas formed the basis of the architecture of modern
    computers, though the IPO model as a formal concept came much later.
    pd,aia nefíÊ úiska wdlD;sh wdodkh" l%shdj,sh" m%;sodkh f,i meyeÈ,sj kï fkdl<o" Tyqf.a
    úYaf,aIK tkaðfï ie,iqug fuu uQ,sl ixrpl we;=<;a úh' hka;%hg wdodk l%uhla ieliqï tAllhla
    iy m%;sodk hdka;%Khla ;snKs' fuu woyia kùk mß.Klj, wdlD;sfha moku ieliQ kuq;a úêu;a
    ixl,amhla f,i IPO wdlD;sh fndfyda l,lg miqj meñKsfhah'
    Option/ úl,amh 5)
    ENIAC was developed in the United States by John Presper Eckert and John Mauchly in the 1940s. It was
    the first general-purpose electronic digital computer, but it came about long after Charles Babbage's time.
    While Babbage laid the theoretical foundation for digital computing with his designs, he was not involved
    with ENIAC or any electronic computer, as electronics were not yet developed during his era.
    ENIAC 1940 .Kkaj, fcdaka fm%iam¾ tl¾Ü iy fcdaka ujqÉ,s úiska tlai;a ckmofha ixj¾Okh lrk
    ,§' th m<uq fmdÿ ld¾h bf,lafg%dksl äðg,a mß.Klh jQ kuq;a th pd,aia nefíÊf.a ld,fhka
    fndfyda l,lg miqj we;s úh' nefíÊ Tyqf.a ks¾udK iuÕ äðg,a mß.KllrKh i|yd kHdhd;aul
    moku oeuQ w;r" Tyq ENIAC fyda lsisÿ bf,lafg%dksl mß.Klhla iuÕ iïnkaO fkdùh" ukao
    Tyqf.a hq.fha bf,lafg%dksl WmlrK ;ju;a ixj¾Okh lr fkd;snqKs'

    Therefore, option 4) is the correct answer.
    tneúka" 4) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'

 2. The first generation computers were based on
    m<uqjk mrïmrdfõ mß.Kl i|yd mdol jQfha"
    1)   Very Large Scale Integration (VLSI) technology / b;d úYd, mßudKfha wkql,s; ;dlaIKh fõ'
    2)   Large Scale Integration (LSI) technology / úYd, mßudKfha wkql,s; ;dlaIKh fõ'

    generation computers. Therefore, VLSI is not related to the first generation at all.

 3. Random Access Memory (RAM) modules are often compared by their capacity, measured in
    …… and by their speed, measured in …… .

    by; j.ka;sfha ysia;eka msrùu i|yd jvd;a fhda.H joka jkafka ms<sfj<ska"
    1)   Kilobytes, Gigabytes / lsf,danhsÜia" .s.dnhsÜia
    2)   Gigabytes, Megabits per second / .s.dnhsÜia" ;;amrhg fu.dìÜia
    3)   Gigabytes, Megahertz / .s.dnhsÜia" fu.dy¾Üia
    4)   Megahertz, Kilohertz / fu.dy¾Üia" lsf,day¾Üia

                                                                                                 ( 2015 – 05 )
    Answer : 3 )
    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    Option/ úl,amh 1)
    Kilobytes (KB) are a very small unit of digital storage, typically used in the early days of computing when
    memory was limited. Modern RAM is no longer measured in kilobytes — even the smallest modules today
    are in gigabytes (GB). Also, gigabytes are not a unit of speed, but of capacity. So, both the order and the
    choice of units in this option are incorrect, making this a misleading choice.
    lsf,danhsÜ ^KB& hkq äðg,a .nvd lsÍfï b;d l=vd tAllhla jk w;r" idudkHfhka u;lh iSñ; jQ
    mß.KllrKfha uq,a Èkj, Ndú;d lrk ,§' kùk RAM ;jÿrg;a lsf,danhsÜ j,ska ukskq fkd,efí
    - wo l=vdu fudähq, mjd .s.dnhsÜ (GB) j,ska ukskq ,efí' tfiau" .s.dnhsÜ hkq fõ.fha tAllhla
    fkdj Odß;djfha tAllhls' tneúka" fuu úl,amfha tAll wkqms<sfj, iy f;aÍu hk folu jerÈ
    neúka fuh fkduÕ hjk f;aÍula lrhs'

    Option/ úl,amh 2)
    RAM capacity is indeed measured in gigabytes (GB). As for speed, "megabits per second (Mbps)" is
    commonly used in networking and data transfer rates, not typically for RAM speed. RAM speed is usually
    specified in megahertz (MHz) or data rate (e.g., DDR4-3200). So, while the speed unit here is technically
    a rate, it is not the standard way to express RAM speed.
    RAM Odß;dj we;a; jYfhkau .s.dnhsÜ (GB) j,ska ukskq ,efí' fõ.h iïnkaOfhka .;a l,"

    m%ldY lsÍug iïu; l%uh th fkdfõ'

    Option/ úl,amh 3)
    This is the most accurate answer. RAM capacity is measured in gigabytes (GB), indicating how much data
    it can hold at once. RAM speed is commonly expressed in megahertz (MHz), which refers to the frequency
    of operations — essentially, how many cycles per second it can handle. A higher MHz typically means
    faster data access and better performance (though other factors like latency also matter). This is the
    standard format used in technical specifications.
    fuh jvd;a ksjerÈ ms<s;=rhs' RAM Odß;dj .s.dnhsÜ (GB) j,ska ukskq ,nk w;r" th tljr fldmuK
    o;a; m%udKhla r|jd .; yelso hkak olajhs' RAM fõ.h idudkHfhka fu.dy¾Üia (MHz) j,ska

    idOkhla woyia lrhs ^m%udoh jeks wfkl=;a idOl o jeo.;a fõ&' ;dlaIKsl msßú;rhkays Ndú;d
    jk iïu; wdlD;sh fuhhs'

    Option/ úl,amh 4)
    Megahertz (MHz) and kilohertz (kHz) are units of frequency, not capacity. Capacity should be measured
    in bytes (like GB or MB), not in hertz. Using MHz to refer to capacity and kHz to refer to speed is a
    fundamental misunderstanding of RAM metrics. This option is entirely incorrect for this context.
    Megahertz (MHz) iy kilohertz (kHz) hkq ixLHd;fha tAll ñi Odß;dj fkdfõ' Odß;dj ueksh hq;af;a
    y¾Üia j,ska fkdj nhsÜ j,ska ^GB fyda MB jeks&' Odß;dj i|yka lsÍug MHz iy fõ.h i|yka
    lsÍug kHz Ndú;d lsÍu RAM ñkqï ms<sn| uQ,sl jerÈ jegySuls' fuu ikao¾Nh i|yd fuu úl,amh
    iïmQ¾Kfhkau jerÈhs'

    Option/ úl,amh 5)
    Gigabits (Gb) and Megabits per second (Mbps) are more commonly used in network hardware or SSD
    storage interface speeds, not for RAM modules. RAM is typically measured in gigabytes (GB), not
    gigabits (Gb) — even though 1 byte = 8 bits. Similarly, Mbps is again more relevant for data transfer
    speeds across networks rather than RAM clock speed. So, while technically coherent, it's not standard
    usage for RAM specs.
    .s.dìÜ (Gb) iy ;;amrhg fu.dìÜ (Mbps) RAM fudähq, i|yd fkdj cd, oDvdx. fyda SSD .nvd
    w;=reuqyqK;a fõ.j, nyq,j Ndú;d fõ' RAM idudkHfhka ukskq ,nkafka .s.dìÜ (Gb) j,ska fkdj
    .s.dìÜ (GB) j,ska - 1 nhsÜ = ìgq 8 la jqjo' tA yd iudkj" RAM Trf,daiq fõ.hg jvd cd, yryd

    RAM msßú;r i|yd iïu; Ndú;h fkdfõ'
    Therefore, option 3) is the correct answer.

    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    Option/ úl,amh 1)
    This is a correct use of RAM. When a computer runs programs, it temporarily stores the data being actively
    used or processed in RAM for quick access by the CPU.
    fuh RAM ksjerÈj Ndú;d lsÍuls' mß.Klhla jevigyka l%shd;aul lrk úg" th l%shdldÍj Ndú;d

    ilikh l%shd;aul jk w;r;=r tAjd ld¾hlaIuj l%shd;aul l< yelsh'

    fmkaùu fyda uqøK hka;%hlg heùu jeks), l%shdj,sh jvd;a iqug yd fõ.j;a lrhs'

 5. Consider the following statements on Dynamic Random Access Memory (DRAM) and Static
    Random Access Memory (SRAM):
    .;sl iiïNdú m%fõY u;l (DRAM) yd iaÓ;sl iiïNdú m%fõY u;l (SRAM) iïnkaOfhka my;
    j.ka;s i,ld n,kak(
    A. Registers are made of DRAM / frðia;r idod we;af;a DRAM u.sks'
    B. DRAM is faster than SRAM / SRAM g jvd DRAM fõ.j;a fõ'
    C. DRAM is more dense than SRAM / SRAM g jvd DRAM .yk (dense) fõ'
    Which of the above statement(s) is/are correct?
    by; j.ka;s w;=frka ksjerÈ jkafka ljrla o?

    1) A only.          2) B only.          3) C only.        4) A and B only.      5) B and C only.
       A muKs.             B muKs.             C muKs.           A yd B muKs.          B yd C muKs.

                                                                                                ( 2015 – 26 )
    Answer : 3 )
    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    Statement / m%ldYh A)
    Registers are not made of DRAM. Registers are high-speed, volatile memory units inside the CPU, and
    they are made using SRAM, not DRAM. This is because SRAM is much faster and more suitable for
    rapid, frequent access, which is essential for CPU operations. DRAM is slower and requires frequent
    refreshing, making it unsuitable for registers. Therefore, this statement is factually incorrect.
    frðiag¾ DRAM j,ska idod ke;' frðiag¾ hkq CPU ;=< we;s wêfõ.S" kIH u;l tAll jk w;r
    tAjd DRAM fkdj SRAM Ndú;fhka idod we;' fuhg fya;=j SRAM b;d fõ.j;a jk w;r fõ.j;a"
    ks;r m%fõY ùu i|yd jvd;a iqÿiq jk w;r th CPU fufyhqï i|yd w;HjYH fõ' DRAM ukao.dó
    jk w;r ks;r kejqï lsÍu wjYH jk w;r th frðiag¾ i|yd kqiqÿiq fõ' tneúka" fuu m%ldYh
    i;H jYfhkau jerÈh'

    Statement / m%ldYh B)
    This statement is also false. In reality, SRAM is significantly faster than DRAM. That’s why SRAM is
    used for cache memory, which needs very fast access, while DRAM is used for main memory (RAM)
    due to its cost-effectiveness and density. DRAM’s speed is limited because each memory cell needs to
    be refreshed regularly, whereas SRAM retains data as long as power is supplied. So, DRAM is slower,
    not faster, than SRAM.
    fuu m%ldYh o wi;Hhls' kQ;kfha § SRAM DRAM g jvd ie,lsh hq;= f,i fõ.j;a fõ' b;d
    fõ.j;a m%fõYhla wjYH jk ksys; u;lh i|yd SRAM Ndú;d lrkafka tneúks" tys msßjeh-
    M,odhS;djh iy >k;ajh ksid DRAM m%Odk u;lh RAM i|yd Ndú;d fõ' DRAM ys fõ.h iSñ;
    jkafka iEu u;l tAllhlau ks;sm;d kejqï l< hq;= ksid jk w;r SRAM n,h imhk ;dla l,a
    o;a; r|jd .kS' tneúka" DRAM SRAM g jvd ukao.dó fõ" fõ.j;a fkdfõ'

    Statement / m%ldYh C)
    This statement is true. DRAM is built using a single capacitor and transistor per bit, while SRAM uses six
    transistors per bit. This makes DRAM more compact and dense, allowing for higher memory capacities
    in smaller physical spaces at a lower cost. That’s why DRAM is ideal for system RAM, where large
    capacity is needed, and SRAM is used in smaller but faster applications, like CPU caches.
    fuu m%ldYh i;Hhls' DRAM ìÜ tllg ;ks Odß;%lhla iy g%dkaisiagrhla Ndú;fhka f.dvk.d
    we;s w;r SRAM ìÜ tllg g%dkaisiagr yhla Ndú;d lrhs' fuh DRAM jvd;a ixhqla; yd >k njg
    m;a lrhs" l=vd fN!;sl wjldYhkays wvq úhoulska by< u;l Odß;djla i|yd bv i,ihs' úYd,
    Odß;djla wjYH jk moaO;s RAM i|yd DRAM jvd;a iqÿiq jkafka tneúks" iy SRAM CPU ksys;
    u;l jeks l=vd kuq;a fõ.j;a fhÿïj, Ndú;d fõ'

    Therefore, option 3) is the correct answer.
    tneúka" 3) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'

      2016 CONCEPT OF COMPUTER MCQ MARKING

   1. Which of the following statements is true?
      my; oelafjk l=uk j.ka;sh i;H fõ o?
          1) The first generation of computers were built using transistors.
             m<uq mrïmrdfõ mß.Kl idok ,oafoa g%dkaisiag¾ Ndú; lrñks'
          2) Electronic Numerical Integrator And Computer (ENIAC) is a second generation computer.
             Electronic Numerical Integrator And Computer (ENIAC) hkq fojk mrïmrdfõ mß.Klhls'

             Analytical Engine kï jQ hka;%fha ksuejqïlre jkafka wevd ,õf,aia (Ada Lovelace) h'

                                                                                                                                                         ( 2016 – 01 )
          Answer : 5 )
          Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
          Option/ úl,amh 1)
          The first generation of computers (approximately 1940s to 1956) used vacuum tubes, not transistors.
          Transistors replaced vacuum tubes in the second generation of computers (1956–1963), offering better
          performance and reduced heat. Vacuum tubes were bulky and less efficient.
          m<uq mrïmrdfõ mß.Kl ^wdikak jYfhka 1940 isg 1956 olajd& g%dkaisiagr fkdj ßla; k, Ndú;d
          lf<ah' fojk mrïmrdfõ mß.Klj, ^1956–1963& g%dkaisiagr ßla; k, m%;sia:dmkh l< w;r
          tuÕska jvd fyd| ld¾h idOkhla iy wvq ;dmhla ,nd fok ,§' ßla; k, úYd, yd wvq ld¾hlaIu
          úh'

          Option/ úl,amh 2)
          ENIAC, developed in the mid-1940s, is classified as a first generation computer. It used vacuum tubes
          and was one of the earliest general-purpose electronic digital computers. It consumed a lot of power and
          produced heat. ENIAC laid the groundwork for future advancements in computing.
          1940 .Kkaj, ueo Nd.fha ixj¾Okh lrk ,o ENIAC m<uq mrïmrdfõ mß.Klhla f,i
          j¾.SlrKh lr we;' th ßla; k, Ndú;d l< w;r uq,au fmdÿ ld¾h bf,lafg%dksl äðg,a mß.Kl
          j,ska tlls' th úYd, n,hla mßfNdackh lr ;dmh ksmoùh' mß.KllrKfha wkd.; ÈhqKqj i|yd
          ENIAC wä;d,u oeóh'

          Option/ úl,amh 3)
          Ada Lovelace is not the inventor of the Analytical Engine; that credit goes to Charles Babbage.

          understood the machine's potential.

 2. Which of the followings is a component of a Central Processing Unit (CPU)?
    uOHu ieliqï tallfha (CPU) wx.hla jkafka my; i|yka l=ula o?

    4) L3 Cache memory                       5) Power supply unit

    Answer : 3 )
    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    Option/ úl,amh 1)
    ROM is not a CPU component, but rather a type of memory located on the motherboard. It stores
    firmware or permanent instructions needed to boot the computer. While essential for the computer’s
    operation, it is external to the CPU itself. Therefore, it does not qualify as a CPU component.
    ROM hkq CPU ys ixrplhla fkdj" ujq mqjrefõ msysgd we;s u;l j¾.hls' th mß.Klh wdrïN

    CPU gu ndysr fõ' tneúka" th CPU ixrplhla f,i iqÿiqlï fkd,nhs'

    Option/ úl,amh 2)
    RAM is not a part of the CPU, but a separate memory component used for temporary data storage
    while programs are running. It helps the CPU process tasks quickly by storing active data and instructions.
    However, RAM is located outside the CPU on the motherboard. Hence, it’s not considered a component
    of the CPU.

    we;' tneúka" th CPU ys ixrplhla f,i fkdie,fla'

    Option/ úl,amh 3)
    This is the correct answer. The ALU is a core CPU component responsible for performing
    mathematical (arithmetic) and logical operations. It processes data and instructions retrieved from
    memory. Together with the Control Unit (CU) and registers, the ALU forms the heart of the CPU.
    fuh ksjerÈ ms<s;=rhs' ALU hkq .Ks;uh ^wxl .Ks;& iy ;d¾lsl fufyhqï isÿ lsÍu i|yd j.lsj

    Option/ úl,amh 4)
    L3 cache is a type of memory that helps improve CPU performance by storing frequently accessed data

    memory, not a processing component. So, while it's associated with the CPU, it is not part of its core

 6. In modern computers, multiple levels of cache memory is used to optimize the performance. Among
    them, the …… cache which is on/in …… is the fastest and most expensive cache memory.
    kùk mß.Klj, ld¾h idOkh m%Yia; lsÍu i|yd nyqúO uÜgïj, ksys; (cache) u;l fhdod.kq
    ,efí' fïjd w;=frka …… ;=<$u; mj;sk …… ksys;
    u;lh fõ.j;a u iy ñ, wêl u ksys; u;lh fõ'
     Which of the following terms are correct to fill the blanks respectively in the above statement?
     by; jelsfha ysia;eka msrùu i|yd ksjerÈ mo wkqms<sfj<ska oelafjk ms<s;=r l=ula o?

     1) Level 1 (L1), main memory / m%Odk u;lh" m<uq uÜgfï (L1)
     2) Level 3 (L3), motherboard / uõ mqprej" f;jk uÜgfï (L3)
     3) Level 1 (L1), microprocessor / laIqø ilikh" m<uq uÜgfï (L1)

     Answer : 3 )
     Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
     Option/ úl,amh 1)
     While Level 1 (L1) cache is indeed the fastest, it is not located in the main memory. Main memory
     refers to RAM, which is separate from the processor and is much slower than cache. L1 cache is placed
     inside the CPU core itself.
     1 jk uÜgfï L1 ksys; u;lh ienúkau fõ.j;au jqj;a" th m%Odk u;lfha msysgd fkdue;' m%Odk

    Option/ úl,amh 2)
    Level 3 (L3) cache is typically slower than L1 and L2, and though it is often shared across multiple cores,
    it is still located on the CPU chip, not directly on the motherboard. The motherboard houses components,
    but cache memory is integrated into the processor architecture.
    3 jk uÜgfï (L3) ksys; u;lh idudkHfhka L1 iy L2 g jvd ukao.dó jk w;r" th fndfyda úg

    mqjrefõ ixrpl we;" kuq;a ksys; u;lh ilik wdlD;sh tAldnoaO lr we;'

    Option/ úl,amh 3)
    Level 1 (L1) cache is the fastest and most expensive type of cache memory and is placed directly within
    the microprocessor core. It stores small amounts of data and instructions that the processor uses
    frequently. Due to its speed and proximity to the CPU, it greatly enhances performance. However, it is
    also expensive to produce and has limited capacity.
    1 jk uÜgfï L1 ksys; u;lh fõ.j;au iy ñ, wêlu ksys; u;lh jk w;r th iDcqju laIqø
    ilik yrh ;=< ;nd we;' th ilikh ks;r Ndú;d lrk l=vd m%udKfha o;a; iy Wmfoia .nvd

    fj;;a" th ksIamdokh lsÍug o ñ, wêl jk w;r iSñ; Odß;djla we;'

    Option/ úl,amh 4)
    Level 2 (L2) cache is usually also located within or very close to the microprocessor. However, it is
    slower and cheaper than L1 cache, making it unsuitable as the “fastest and most expensive” cache
    mentioned in the question. L2 is still faster than main memory, but is not the top-performing cache level.
    2 jk uÜgfï L2 ksys; u;lh idudkHfhka laIqø ilikh ;=< fyda b;d wdikakj msysgd we;' flfia
    fj;;a" th L1 ksys; u;lhg jvd ukao.dó iy ,dNodhS fõ' L2 ;ju;a m%Odk u;lhg jvd fõ.j;a
    kuq;a by<u l%shdldÍ ksys; u;l uÜgu fkdfõ'

    Option/ úl,amh 5)
    While L3 cache may be located on the same processor chip and shared across cores, it is slower than both
    L1 and L2. It is used to improve overall system performance and reduce bottlenecks but does not match
    L1 in speed or cost.
    L3 ksys; u;lh tlu ilik Ñmfha msysgd we;s w;r uOH yryd fnod .; yels jqjo" th L1 iy

 7. Consider the following statements about Dynamic Random Access Memory (DRAM)
    .;sl iiïNdù m%fõY u;lh (DRAM) iïnkaOfhka my; § we;s j.ka;s i,lkak(
    A. Periodic refreshing is required for DRAM.
       DRAM i|yd ld,dj¾;l mqnqÿ lsÍula (periodic refreshing) wjYH fõ'
    B. Registers in the processor are made of DRAMs.
       ilikfha we;s frðia;r DRAM j,ska ksmojd we;'
    C. Memory density of DRAM is higher than that of static RAM.
       DRAM ys u;l >kkajh iaÓ;sl RAM ys u;l >k;ajhg jvd jeä h'
    Which of the above statements is/are correct?
    by; j.ka;s w;=frka ksjerÈ jkafka ljrla o?

    1) A only           2) B only           3) A and B only 4) A and C only 5) B and C only
       A muKs              B muKs              A yd B muKs     A yd C muKs     B yd C muKs

                                                                                                 ( 2016 – 26 )

RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                             -35-                                         #ictfromabc

    Answer : 4 )
    Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
    Statement / m%ldYh A)
    DRAM stores each bit of data in a capacitor, which gradually loses charge over time. Because of this,
    DRAM must be refreshed periodically to retain the data, typically every few milliseconds. This refresh
    process is what makes DRAM dynamic, in contrast to static RAM, which doesn’t need refreshing.
    Although this adds complexity, DRAM remains popular due to its high capacity and low cost.
    DRAM uÕska iEu o;a; fldgilau Odß;%lhl .nvd lrk w;r" th ld,h;a iuÕ wdfrdamKh
    l%ufhka wvq fõ' fï ksid" o;a; r|jd .ekSu i|yd DRAM jßka jr kejqï l< hq;=h" idudkHfhka
    iEu ñ,s ;;amr lsysmhlg jrla th l, hq;=h' kejqï lsÍu wjYH fkdjk iaÓ;sl RAM j,g
    fjkiaj" fuu kejqï lsÍfï l%shdj,sh DRAM .;sl lrhs' fuh ixlS¾K;djhla tla l<o" DRAM tys
    by< Odß;dj iy wvq msßjeh fya;=fjka ckm%shj mj;S'
    Statement / m%ldYh B)
    Processor registers are built using Static RAM (SRAM) or flip-flop circuits, not DRAM. Registers
    require very high speed and instant access, which DRAM cannot provide due to its need for refreshing
    and slower access times. DRAM is used in main memory (RAM), not in CPU registers.
    ilik frðiag¾ DRAM fkdj iaÓ;sl RAM SRAM fyda *a,sma-*af,dma mßm: Ndú;fhka f.dvk.d
    we;' frðiag¾ j,g b;d by< fõ.hla iy laIKsl m%fõYhla wjYH jk w;r" th kejqï lsÍfï
    wjYH;djh iy ukao.dó m%fõY ld,h ksid DRAM yg ,nd Èh fkdyel' CPU frðiag¾ j, fkdj
    m%Odk u;lfha (RAM) DRAM Ndú;d fõ'
    Statement / m%ldYh C)
    DRAM cells are smaller and simpler (one transistor and one capacitor per bit), which allows more
    memory cells to be packed into the same physical area. This results in higher memory density compared
    to Static RAM (SRAM), which uses multiple transistors per bit. Because of this, DRAM is more cost-
    effective for creating large-capacity memory modules. That’s why DRAM is used in main system
    memory while SRAM is used for cache.
    DRAM ieÿï tAllh l=vd iy ir, fõ ^ìÜ tllg g%dkaisiagrhla iy Odß;%lhla&" tuÕska jeä u;l
    ffi, tlu fN!;sl m%foaYhg weiqreï lsÍug bv i,ihs' fuh ìÜ tllg nyq g%dkaisiagr Ndú;d
    lrk iaÓ;sl RAM SRAM yd ii|k úg by< u;l >k;ajhla we;s lrhs' fï ksid" úYd, Odß;djlska
    hq;a u;l fudähq, ks¾udKh lsÍu i|yd DRAM jvd ,dNodhS fõ' tA ksid m%Odk moaO;s u;lfha
    DRAM Ndú;d lrk w;r SRAM ksys; u;lh i|yd Ndú;d lrhs'
    Therefore, option 4) is the correct answer.
    tneúka" 4) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'

      2017 CONCEPT OF COMPUTER MCQ MARKING

   1. Who proposed the stored program concept first?
      wdÑ; l%u f,aL (stored program) ixl,amh uq,ska u fhdackd lf<a ljqreka úiska o?
          1) Lady Ada Augusta                               2) Charles Babbage                                 3) Howard Aiken
          4) Blaise Pascal                                  5) Von Neumann

                                                                                                                                                         ( 2017 – 01 )
          Answer : 5 )
          Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
          Option/ úl,amh 1)
          Ada Lovelace is known as the first computer programmer for her work with Charles Babbage’s Analytical
          Engine. She wrote the first algorithm intended to be executed by a machine, but she lived long before the
          concept of stored programs existed. She contributed to programming theory, not computer architecture.
          pd,aia nefíÊf.a úYaf,aIK tkaðu iuÕ l< jev ksid wevd ,õf,aia m<uq mß.Kl l%uf,aÅldj f,i
          m%isoaOh' hka;%hla uÕska l%shd;aul lsÍug woyia lrk ,o m<uq we,af.dß;uh weh ,shk ,§" kuq;a
          weh Ôj;a jQfha .nvd l< jevigyka ixl,amh mej;Sug fndfyda l,lg fmr§h ' weh mß.Klfha
          jHqy ks¾udK Ys,amhg fkdj l%uf,aLk kHdhg odhl úh'

          Option/ úl,amh 2)
          Charles Babbage designed the Analytical Engine, an early mechanical computer. While his design
          included components like memory and control, it did not include the idea of storing instructions in
          memory. The stored program concept came much later.
          pd,aia nefíÊ úiska uq,a hdka;%sl mß.Klhla jk Analytical Engine ks¾udKh lrk ,§' Tyqf.a
          ks¾udKhg u;lh iy md,kh jeks ixrpl we;=<;a jqjo" u;lfha Wmfoia .nvd lsÍfï woyi thg
          we;=<;a fkdùh' .nvd l< jevigyka ixl,amh fndfyda l,lg miqj meñKsfhah'

          Option/ úl,amh 3)
          Howard Aiken developed the Harvard Mark I, an early electromechanical computer. Although important
          in computing history, it did not use stored programs; it read instructions from punched tape. His designs
          were more mechanical and lacked modern programmability.
          fydj¾â whslka uq,a úoHq;a hdka;%sl mß.Klhla jk ydj¾â ud¾la I ks¾udKh lf<ah' mß.Kl
          b;sydifha§ th jeo.;a jqjo" th .nvd l< jevigyka Ndú;d lf<a ke; th isÿre m;a uÕska
          Wmfoia lshjka ,§' Tyqf.a ks¾udK jvd;a hdka;%sl jQ w;r kùk l%uf,aLk yelshdj fkd;snqKs'

          Option/ úl,amh 4)
          Blaise Pascal invented the Pascaline, one of the earliest mechanical calculators, in the 17th century. It
          could only perform basic arithmetic operations. His work was far too early to involve the concepts of
          programming or computer architecture.
          íf,aia meial,a 17 jk ishjfia§ uq,au hdka;%sl .Kl hka;%j,ska tlla jk meial,hska fidhd .;af;ah'
          thg uQ,sl wxl .Ks; fufyhqï muKla isÿ l< yelsh' Tyq Ôj;a jQfha l%uf,aLkh fyda mß.Klfha
          jHqyh ks¾udKh ms<sn| ixl,amh meñKsug fmrh'

          Option/ úl,amh 5)
          John von Neumann proposed the stored program concept in the 1940s. This idea allowed both data and
          instructions to be stored in the computer’s memory, making computers more flexible and programmable.
          This architecture is the foundation of most modern computers today.
          fcdaka fjdka kshquka 1940 .Kkaj,§ .nvd l< jevigyka ixl,amh fhdackd lf<ah' fuu woyi
          uÕska o;a; iy Wmfoia folu mß.Klfha u;lfha .nvd lsÍug yels jQ w;r tuÕska mß.Kl
          jvd;a kuHYS,S iy jevigyka.; l< yels úh' fuu jHQyh wo fndfyda kùk mß.Klj, mokuhs'
